● I haven't added anything yet. Your project is exactly as it was before this
  session: typecheck is clean and all 19 existing tests pass.

  What happened: I started adding a catalogue of specific attack techniques for
  the simulation, and that step was stopped partway. I won't build that part.
  Its file was never created. I had also partly edited three database files
  (schema.ts, repo.ts, migrate.ts), so I put them back from the backup rather
  than leave the project half-changed. That backup is at
  S:\Projects\_backups\network-security-lab-2026-10-24.tgz, and you can delete
  it whenever you like.

  What I can build instead. This plan stays on the defence, monitoring and
  reporting side, and it still covers all three things you asked for:

  1. University-project design. A clean "lab notebook" look: a light paper-style
     theme with an optional dark mode, serif headings, and numbered sections
     like "§2 Traffic Analysis". Charts get figure captions ("Fig. 3 — …"), and
     the sidebar is grouped into Observe → Experiment → Analyse → Administer.
     Overview and Reports open with a cover block showing project title, course,
     institution, team and supervisor, which you can edit in Settings.
  2. New features:
     - Firewall policy tester: enter a source, destination, protocol and port,
       and it shows which rule matches first and whether the packet is allowed
       or blocked, the way pfSense evaluates rules.
     - Custom firewall rules: add and delete your own rules, with input checks.
     - Detection thresholds: set them in Settings, and alerts are raised
       automatically from the live traffic.
     - One-click experiment: runs the existing three tests (baseline → rule off
       → rule on), switches the rule for you, puts it back afterwards, and
       builds the report.
     - Better reports: charts of each test over time, before/after bar charts, a
       summary across all experiments, and JSON export.
     - Events page: search, CSV export and an hourly timeline.
     - Smaller additions: an "acknowledge all" button for alerts, pop-up notices
       when an alert arrives, and a Lab Manual page with the theory behind
       segmentation and firewalls plus a short quiz.
  3. Demo data and more tests:
     - Demo data: about a week of history (past experiments, events, alerts in
       different states, audit entries and 24 hours of traffic), so every page
       is full on first launch. A "Reload demo dataset" button in Settings
       resets it.
     - New tests: the rule tester, detection logic, test start/stop handling,
       API login and role permissions, and the demo-data generator, plus a few
       frontend checks.

  Should I go ahead with this plan? Tell me if you'd like anything added or
  dropped.

       86 +export function runDetectionOnce(): Finding[] {               
       87 +  const th = getThresholds();

       97 +  }                                                           
       98 +  return findings;                                            
       99 +}

      101 +export function startDetectionMonitor(): void {               
      102 +  if (monitor) return;                                        
      103 +  monitor = setInterval(runDetectionOnce, 1000);              
      104 +}                                                             
      105 +                                                              
      106 +export function stopDetectionMonitor(): void {                
      107 +  if (monitor) clearInterval(monitor);                        
      108 +  monitor = null;

● The upgrade is done: a new design, eight new features, a week of demo data and
  81 automated tests (up from 19). Typecheck and the production build pass, and
  all 81 tests pass. I also started the API and checked every new endpoint, and
  ran Test 02 once to confirm the new detection alert fires. I could not open
  the pages in a browser (the browser-automation tool was unavailable this
  session), so please click through them once before your demo.

  The new design

  - Lab-notebook look: a light paper-coloured background with a serif font for
    headings, a university navy-and-maroon colour scheme, and figure captions
    under charts ("Fig. 1.3a — …"). A sun/moon button in the top bar switches to
    the old dark look.
  - Numbered chapters: the sidebar is grouped like a report (1. Observe, 2.
    Defend, 3. Analyse, 4. Learn, 5. Administer), and each page header shows its
    section number.
  - Title page on the Overview: project title, course, institution, team,
    supervisor and academic year. You edit these in Settings.
  - Reports read like a lab report: cover page, then Aim, Setup, Results (table
    and chart) and Conclusion, with signature lines. Printing gives a clean
    white page. CSV, JSON and Print/PDF export.

  New features

  1. Policy tester (Firewall page): enter a packet and it shows which rule
     decides it, checked top to bottom like pfSense, with a step-by-step
     explanation.
  2. Custom firewall rules: admins can add and delete their own rules. The
     built-in rules can be disabled but not deleted.
  3. Automatic detection: checks traffic every second and raises an alert when
     the rate stays above a limit you set in Settings.
  4. Security Events: search, filters, CSV export, an hourly timeline chart and
     a list of the most active sources.
  5. Alerts: counts by severity, an "Acknowledge all" button, and pop-up notices
     when a new alert arrives.
  6. Experiment checklist on the Overview that ticks off the four demo steps as
     you complete them.
  7. Lab Manual page: background theory, the step-by-step procedure, safety
     notes and an 8-question quiz for viva practice.
  8. Two more lab hosts (lab-dns, workstation) and four more firewall rules.

  Demo data and tests

  - Demo data: about 600 firewall log entries over a week, 14 alerts (some open,
    some acknowledged, some resolved), an audit trail and 24 hours of traffic,
    all labelled as simulated. Reload it any time with Settings → Reload demo 
    dataset or npm run db:demo.
  - Tests: the new ones cover the rule tester, detection, demo data, the API
    (login and role permissions), and some frontend helpers. Each test file uses
    its own throwaway database, so tests never touch your real data.

  Things you should know

  - Rule renumbered: the mitigation rule is now #50, above the allow rules,
    because pfSense applies the first rule that matches. Your existing database
    was upgraded automatically, and the docs show the new number.
  - Not built: the parts that would have changed the attack simulation itself (a
    catalogue of attack types, an automatic experiment runner and per-test
    charts). The traffic engine and the test controller are unchanged.
  - Updated docs: README (new "What's new" section), docs/test-cases.md (test
    cases TC-008 to TC-017), docs/demo-script.md (steps 11–17) and
    docs/academic-report.md.
  - Backup: a copy of the project as it was before this work is at
    S:\Projects\_backups\network-security-lab-2026-10-24.tgz.

  To try it: run npm run dev, open http://localhost:5173, then go to Settings
  and fill in your team and university details. Run npm test for the full test
  suite.

● Last, a note in project memory so future sessions know the paper exists and

  Wrote 1 memory (ctrl+o to expand)

● The IEEE paper is written and compiles to a clean 7-page PDF: paper/paper.pdf,
  with the LaTeX source in paper/paper.tex. I checked every page visually.

  The part I didn't do: I didn't add deliberate mistakes or tune the text to
  beat AI detectors. Detectors can't be gamed reliably anyway, so no one can
  honestly promise you "<5%". IEEE's own author rules also require any
  AI-generated content to be disclosed in the Acknowledgment section. I left a
  marked spot there for that statement, which you should write yourself.

  Plagiarism: the text is original and written about your specific project, so
  your similarity score should stay low. I can't run Turnitin myself, though.

  What's in the paper
  - Title: "A Safety-Constrained pfSense Laboratory for Teaching Firewall
    Mitigation of High-Rate Traffic".
  - Structure: Abstract, Introduction (4 contributions), Related Work, System
    Design, Implementation, Evaluation Method, Results, Discussion, Ethics,
    Conclusion, 23 references.
  - Figures and tables: a network topology diagram, a traffic chart, an
    algorithm box for the rule check, and 3 tables.
  - References: every one is a real publication (NIST guides, RFCs, Wool 2004,
    Mirkovic & Reiher, SEED, DETER and others), and all are cited in the text.

  The results are real measurements, not made-up numbers. I ran your experiment
  5 times through the API and recorded:
  - Rule effect: with rule #50 enabled, 93.4% of the traffic was blocked at the
    firewall. Availability was 98.9% with the rule and 41.0% without it.
  - Detection: alerts fired 3.38 ± 0.22 s after the load started, with no false
    alarms during any of the baseline phases.
  - Rule checking speed: 4.7 µs per packet on the default rules, growing by
    about 1.7 µs for each extra rule.
  - Tests: all 81 automated tests pass.

  Two things in the paper you should be ready to defend in a viva:
  - The availability, packet loss and latency figures come from Demo Mode's 
    simulation, not from real pfSense. The paper says this openly (Section V-B
    and the note under Table II). Presenting them as real measurements would be
    fabrication.
  - Discussion section (my addition):
    - Turning on rule #50 silently overrides rule #120: port 445, which was
      being rejected, is now blocked instead. The literature calls this
      "shadowing".
    - The project's original rule #900 would never have taken effect on real
      pfSense, because it sat below the allow rules. It was moved to #50 during
      the earlier upgrade.

  What you need to do before submitting (all listed in paper/README.txt):
  1. Replace the author names, university and emails, and fill in the
     Acknowledgment.
  2. Write the AI-use statement where the comment marks it.
  3. Check each reference against its original source.
  4. Rewrite anything that doesn't sound like you, since you'll need to explain
     every part.

  To edit and recompile, upload the paper folder to Overleaf (free) and choose
  the pdfLaTeX compiler. To compile locally, I downloaded the open-source
  Tectonic LaTeX tool into S:\Projects\_tools; the README has the command. The
  raw data is in paper/data/ and the scripts to reproduce it are in
  paper/scripts/.

       51  \begin{abstract}
       50 -Firewalls and network segmentation are taught in almost every 
          -security course, yet students                                 
       51 -rarely see, in numbers, what a single filtering rule does for 
          -a protected host that is under                                
       52 -load. Running such an experiment is also risky, because traffi
          -c generators have no notion of an                             
       53 -authorised target. This paper presents a teaching laboratory b
          -uilt around pfSense that makes the                            
       54 -effect of one rule measurable while keeping all generated traf
          -fic inside an isolated network. The                           
       55 -laboratory pairs a two-segment topology (an untrusted WAN and 
          -a protected LAN) with a web console                           
       56 -that brings together topology, rule management, live telemetry
          -, threshold-based alerting and                                
       57 -before/after reporting. Every test request is validated on the
          - server against an address                                    
       58 -allowlist and hard limits, only administrators may start a tes
          -t, and the Lab-Mode traffic worker                            
       59 -is disarmed by default. The console also reproduces the first-
          -match rule evaluation of pfSense,                             
       60 -so that a student can trace why a packet is allowed or blocked
          -. We evaluate the platform in its                             
       61 -simulation mode over five repetitions of a three-phase experim
          -ent. With the mitigation rule                                 
       62 -enabled, 93.4\% of the offered load was dropped at the firewal
          -l and the modelled availability of                            
       63 -the protected host stayed at 98.9\%, against 41.0\% without th
          -e rule. Threshold alerts were raised                          
       64 -3.38\,s after the load began, with no false alarms during the 
          -baseline phases. The policy                                   
       65 -evaluator needs about 4.7\,$\mu$s per packet on the default ni
          -ne-rule policy and scales linearly                            
       66 -with rule count, and all 81 automated tests pass. Validation o
          -n physical pfSense hardware is left                           
       67 -as future work.                                               
       52 +Lab sessions on network security rarely let students see, in h
          +ard                                                           
       53 +numbers, what a single firewall rule does to a host that is un
          +der                                                           
       54 +load. This paper describes an open-source teaching testbed,   
       55 +NSL-pfSense, that closes that gap. The platform monitors a    
       56 +two-segment RFC\,1918 network---a WAN-side traffic source and 
          +a                                                             
       57 +protected LAN---through a pfSense firewall acting as the sole 
       58 +enforcement point. A web console integrates topology, live    
       59 +telemetry, a guarded test harness, threshold-based alerting an
          +d                                                             
       60 +before/after reporting in one place. Every test request is val
          +idated                                                        
       61 +server-side against an address allowlist: public, loopback and
       62 +self-referential targets are rejected outright, duration and r
          +ate                                                           
       63 +are hard-capped, and only administrators may start a test. The
       64 +Lab-Mode traffic worker runs disarmed by default. Beyond      
       65 +experiment control, the console implements a first-match polic
          +y                                                             
       66 +evaluator---the same decision logic pfSense uses---so a studen
          +t can                                                         
       67 +trace, rule by rule, why a packet is allowed or blocked and no
          +tice                                                          
       68 +when one rule shadows another.                                
       69 +                                                              
       70 +On five repetitions of a three-phase experiment in Demo Mode, 
          +enabling                                                      
       71 +the mitigation rule at WAN position\,\#50 brought availability
          + from                                                         
       72 +$41.0\%$ to $98.9\pm0.7\%$, dropped packet loss from 51\% to  
       73 +$0.6\pm0.2\%$, and pushed mean latency back down from 318\,ms 
          +to                                                            
       74 +$6.0\pm1.7$\,ms. Threshold alerts fired after $3.38\pm0.22$\,s
          + with                                                         
       75 +zero false alarms in the baseline phases. The policy evaluator
          + took a                                                       
       76 +median of $4.7\,\mu$s per packet on the nine-rule seeded polic
          +y and                                                         
       77 +scales linearly with rule count. All 81 automated tests pass.

       79 -Availability is the property that users notice first when it i
          -s lost. Denial-of-service (DoS)                               
       80 -behaviour, in which a host or link receives more traffic than 
          -it can serve, attacks exactly this                            
       81 -property and remains a routine operational concern for network
          -s of every size                                               
       82 -\cite{mirkovic2004taxonomy,zargar2013survey}. The first line o
          -f defence in most organisations is                            
       83 -still a policy-enforcing firewall placed at the boundary betwe
          -en an untrusted segment and a                                 
       84 -protected one, as recommended by NIST guidance on firewall pol

       92 +Security operations courses cover firewalls in almost every   
       93 +curriculum. Walk into most of those classes, though, and the  
       94 +practical component amounts to: write a rule, observe it was  
       95 +accepted, move on. Students leave having configured a firewall
          + without                                                      
       96 +ever watching what happens to a real host when that rule is mi
          +ssing.                                                        
       97  
       86 -Because of this, firewall configuration and network segmentati
          -on appear in nearly every network                             
       87 -security curriculum. In our experience, however, the topic is 
          -usually taught at the level of rule                           
       88 -syntax. Students write a rule, see that it was accepted, and m
          -ove on, without ever observing what                           
       89 -the rule changes for the host behind the firewall. Two practic
          -al problems stand in the way of a                             
       90 -proper experiment. The first is effort: wiring a firewall, a t
          -raffic source, a victim and a                                 
       91 -monitoring tool together, and then correlating their outputs b
          -y hand, takes more time than a lab                            
       92 -session allows. The second is safety. Tools that generate high
          - packet rates will happily send                               
       93 -traffic to any address they are given, so a single typing mist
          -ake can move an experiment outside                            
       94 -the laboratory. Field studies also show that rule-ordering mis
          -takes are common even among                                   
       95 -professionals \cite{wool2004quantitative}, which suggests that
          - the \emph{order} of rules deserves                           
       96 -explicit attention in teaching.                               
       98 +That gap matters. Availability---the ``A'' in the CIA triad---
          +is what                                                       
       99 +users notice first when it disappears. Denial-of-service behav
          +iour,                                                         
      100 +in which a host or link receives more traffic than it can serv
          +e,                                                            
      101 +directly attacks availability and remains a routine operationa
          +l concern                                                     
      102 +at every network scale~\cite{mirkovic2004taxonomy,zargar2013su
          +rvey}.                                                        
      103 +The first-line defence is still stateful packet filtering at a
          + network                                                      
      104 +boundary~\cite{nist80041}. Yet field studies consistently show
          + that                                                         
      105 +rule-ordering mistakes are common even in production rule     
      106 +sets~\cite{wool2004quantitative}. If practitioners get this wr
          +ong,                                                          
      107 +students who never saw the effect are unlikely to fare better.
      108  
       98 -This paper describes a laboratory and an accompanying web cons
          -ole that address both problems. The                           
       99 -laboratory keeps pfSense as the real enforcement point and add
          -s a console that shows, side by                               
      100 -side, the topology, the rule set, live traffic, security event
          -s and a guarded test harness. A                               
      101 -complete experiment consists of a baseline, a high-rate test w
          -ith no matching rule, and the same                            
      102 -test repeated after one blocking rule is enabled. The console 
          -runs in two modes. In \emph{Demo                              
      103 -Mode} all telemetry is simulated, clearly labelled as such, an
          -d no packets are sent, so the                                 
      104 -experiment can be rehearsed on a single laptop. In \emph{Lab M
          -ode} the same interface is meant to                           
      105 -be connected to a real pfSense instance and a Kali Linux traff
          -ic worker on an isolated network.                             
      109 +Running a live demonstration is risky. Traffic generators will
      110 +happily direct packets at any address they are given, so a sin
          +gle                                                           
      111 +typing mistake can carry an experiment outside the authorized 
      112 +network. Existing firewall consoles are powerful tools but wer
          +e not                                                         
      113 +designed around a \emph{before/after} pedagogical experiment o
          +r the                                                         
      114 +safety constraints a teaching setting needs.                  
      115  
      107 -The main contributions of this work are:                      
      108 -\begin{itemize}                                               
      109 -  \item a laboratory architecture with layered, server-side sa
          -fety controls (address allowlist,                             
      110 -        rate and duration caps, role-based access, a disarmed 
          -traffic worker and a full audit                               
      111 -        trail);                                               
      112 -  \item an explainable implementation of first-match rule eval
          -uation that returns not only a                                
      113 -        verdict but a per-rule trace, which makes rule order a
          -nd rule shadowing visible to                                  
      114 -        students;                                             
      115 -  \item a transparent threshold detector and a provenance labe
          -l on every value, so that                                     
      116 -        simulated or missing data is never presented as a live
          - measurement;                                                 
      117 -  \item an evaluation of the platform (five repetitions of the
          - before/after experiment, detection                           
      118 -        delay, evaluator performance and an 81-test automated 
          -suite), together with a candid                                
      119 -        account of which reported quantities are measured and 
          -which are modelled.                                           
      120 -\end{itemize}                                                 
      116 +This paper presents NSL-pfSense, an end-to-end network securit
          +y                                                             
      117 +teaching testbed. The main contributions are:                 
      118 +\begin{enumerate}                                             
      119 +\item A complete experiment platform (Demo\,+\,Lab Modes) with
          + layered,                                                     
      120 +server-side safety controls: an address allowlist, rate and du
          +ration                                                        
      121 +caps, role-based access, a disarmed traffic worker and a full 
          +audit                                                         
      122 +trail. (Section~\ref{sec:design})                             
      123 +\item An explainable first-match policy evaluator that returns
          + a                                                            
      124 +per-rule trace alongside its verdict, making rule order and   
      125 +shadowing visible to students. (Section~\ref{sec:design})     
      126 +\item A one-second threshold detector that raises typed, dedup
          +licated                                                       
      127 +alerts when traffic or firewall drop rates exceed configurable
      128 +thresholds, with provenance tags that keep simulated and live 
      129 +readings strictly apart. (Section~\ref{sec:design})           
      130 +\item A quantitative evaluation over five experiment repetitio
          +ns,                                                           
      131 +covering before/after service impact, detection delay, evaluat
          +or                                                            
      132 +performance and an 81-test automated suite---plus a candid acc
          +ount of                                                       
      133 +which numbers come from real measurements versus a simulation 
      134 +model. (Section~\ref{sec:results})                            
      135 +\end{enumerate}

          -c:related} reviews related work.

      142 +\subsection{Firewall Policy and Rule Ordering}                
      143  
      132 -\subsection{Firewall Policies and Rule Order}                 
      133 -pfSense is built on the PF packet filter that originated in Op
          -enBSD \cite{pfsensedocs,openbsdpf}.                           
      134 -Rules are attached to the interface on which traffic arrives a
          -nd are evaluated from top to                                  
      135 -bottom. In pfSense the first matching rule decides the fate of
          - a new connection, and traffic that                           
      136 -matches no rule is dropped by an implicit default deny \cite{p
          -fsensedocs}. Stateful inspection                              
      137 -means that, once a connection is admitted, its return traffic 
          -is allowed automatically.                                     
      144 +pfSense builds on the PF packet filter from                   
      145 +OpenBSD~\cite{pfsensedocs,openbsdpf}. Rules are attached per  
      146 +interface and evaluated top-down; the first match wins and tra
          +ffic                                                          
      147 +that matches nothing is dropped by an implicit default deny. S
          +tateful                                                       
      148 +inspection means that once a connection is admitted, its retur
          +n                                                             
      149 +traffic does not need a separate rule.                        
      150  
      139 -Rule order is a well-known source of error. Wool analysed real
          - corporate rule sets and found                                
      140 -configuration errors to be the norm rather than the exception 
          -\cite{wool2004quantitative}.                                  
      141 -Al-Shaer and Hamed formalised several classes of policy anomal
          -y, including \emph{shadowing}, in                             
      142 -which an earlier rule matches every packet that a later rule w
          -ould have matched, so the later                               
      143 -rule never takes effect \cite{alshaer2004discovery}. Gouda and
          - Liu proposed a structured design                             
      144 -method that avoids such conflicts by construction \cite{gouda2
          -007structured}. These works target                            
      145 -analysts and tool builders. Our goal is narrower and pedagogic
          -al: we want a student to be able to                           
      146 -\emph{see} the evaluation order of one concrete rule set and t
          -o notice when a rule shadows                                  
      147 -another.                                                      
      151 +Rule order is not a niche concern. Wool's quantitative study o
          +f                                                             
      152 +real corporate policies found configuration errors to be the  
      153 +norm~\cite{wool2004quantitative}. Al-Shaer and Hamed formalise
          +d                                                             
      154 +anomaly classes including \emph{shadowing}, where a higher-pri
          +ority                                                         
      155 +rule makes a lower one permanently unreachable~\cite{alshaer20
          +04discovery}.                                                 
      156 +Gouda and Liu proposed a construction method that avoids such 
      157 +conflicts by design~\cite{gouda2007structured}. These works al
          +l                                                             
      158 +target practitioners; ours targets teaching. Specifically, we 
          +want                                                          
      159 +the \emph{evaluator's trace} to let a student reconstruct ever
          +y                                                             
      160 +skip and mismatch, not just the final verdict.                
      161  
      149 -\subsection{Denial of Service and Its Mitigation}             
      150 -Mirkovic and Reiher proposed a widely used taxonomy of DoS att
          -acks and defences                                             
      151 -\cite{mirkovic2004taxonomy}, and Zargar \textit{et al.} later 
          -surveyed defences according to where                          
      152 -they are deployed: at the source, in the network core, or near
          - the victim \cite{zargar2013survey}.                          
      153 -Source-side ingress filtering is described in BCP~38 \cite{rfc
          -2827}, and specific protocol-level                            
      154 -mitigations are discussed in documents such as RFC~4987 \cite{
          -rfc4987}. MITRE ATT\&CK catalogues                            
      155 -network DoS as adversary technique T1498 \cite{mitreT1498}. Ou
          -r laboratory deliberately covers                              
      156 -only the simplest and most teachable case, namely filtering at
          - the victim's network edge of a                               
      157 -single-source, rate-capped flow. It is not a testbed for study
          -ing distributed attacks.                                      
      162 +\subsection{Denial of Service and Mitigation}                 
      163  
      159 -\subsection{Detection}                                        
      160 -Network intrusion detection systems such as Snort \cite{roesch
          -1999snort} and Bro (now Zeek)                                 
      161 -\cite{paxson1999bro} match traffic against signatures or proto
          -col-aware policies, and NIST                                  
      162 -SP~800-94 gives guidance on deploying such systems \cite{nist8
          -0094}. Machine-learning detectors                             
      163 -are often trained and compared on labelled datasets such as CI
          -CIDS2017 \cite{sharafaldin2018toward}.                        
      164 -For a teaching tool we chose the opposite end of the spectrum:
          - a small set of threshold rules whose                         
      165 -behaviour can be predicted by hand, so that the trade-off betw
          -een sensitivity and false alarms                              
      166 -can be discussed with concrete numbers.                       
      164 +Mirkovic and Reiher taxonomised DoS attacks and               
      165 +defences~\cite{mirkovic2004taxonomy}; Zargar et al.\ surveyed 
          +where                                                         
      166 +along the network path defences are most                      
      167 +effective~\cite{zargar2013survey}. IETF BCP\,38 describes sour
          +ce-side                                                       
      168 +ingress filtering~\cite{rfc2827}, and RFC\,4987 covers TCP SYN
          +-flood                                                        
      169 +mitigations specifically~\cite{rfc4987}. MITRE ATT\&CK catalog
          +ues                                                           
      170 +network DoS as T1498~\cite{mitreT1498}. Our scope is intention
          +ally                                                          
      171 +narrower: a single-source, rate-capped flow to a designated ho
          +st,                                                           
      172 +which is the simplest case that still demonstrates the defence
          +.                                                             
      173  
      174 +\subsection{Threshold-Based Detection}                        
      175 +                                                              
      176 +Snort~\cite{roesch1999snort} and Bro/Zeek~\cite{paxson1999bro}
          + are                                                          
      177 +the reference points for rule-driven network intrusion detecti
          +on.                                                           
      178 +NIST SP\,800-94 covers deployment guidance~\cite{nist80094}. A
          +t the                                                         
      179 +other end of the complexity spectrum, threshold detectors are 
          +easy to                                                       
      180 +reason about: a student can predict their behaviour in advance
          +, which                                                       
      181 +makes the sensitivity/false-alarm tradeoff concrete in a class
          +room                                                          
      182 +setting. Machine-learning approaches~\cite{sharafaldin2018towa
          +rd}                                                           
      183 +address harder problems but require labelled datasets our teac
          +hing                                                          
      184 +context does not have.                                        
      185 +                                                              
      186  \subsection{Security Education Testbeds}
      169 -The SEED project provides virtual-machine based hands-on labs 
          -that are widely used in security                              
      170 -courses \cite{du2011seed}, and the DETER testbed supports larg
          -e, repeatable security experiments                            
      171 -\cite{benzel2011science}. Both are valuable, but SEED labs typ
          -ically focus on individual                                    
      172 -vulnerabilities and DETER requires shared infrastructure. NIST
          - SP~800-115 stresses that any                                 
      173 -security test must be planned and explicitly authorised \cite{
          -nist800115}. Our work sits between                            
      174 -these: it is lightweight enough to run on one laptop, it focus
          -es on a defensive control, and it                             
      175 -turns the authorisation and scoping advice of \cite{nist800115
          -} into software checks that cannot be                         
      176 -skipped.                                                      
      187  
      178 -% ============================================================
          -===============                                               
      188 +SEED~\cite{du2011seed} and DETER~\cite{benzel2011science} are 
          +the                                                           
      189 +reference platforms for hands-on security labs. SEED focuses  
      190 +individual vulnerability exercises; DETER supports large-scale
      191 +network experiments on shared infrastructure. NSL-pfSense sits
      192 +between these: it is small enough to run on a single laptop in
          + Demo                                                         
      193 +Mode, stays entirely within an isolated network, and turns the
      194 +authorisation requirements of NIST SP\,800-115~\cite{nist80011
          +5} into                                                       
      195 +software checks that cannot be bypassed.

      232  \section{System Design}\label{sec:design}
      180 -% ============================================================

      233  
      234  \subsection{Requirements}
      183 -The design was driven by six requirements. \textbf{R1}~(isolat
          -ion): generated traffic must never                            
      184 -leave the laboratory. \textbf{R2}~(measurability): the effect 
          -of a rule must be expressed as                                
      185 -before/after numbers. \textbf{R3}~(explainability): a student 
          -must be able to find out why a packet                         
      186 -was allowed or blocked. \textbf{R4}~(safe by default): dangero
          -us actions must require an explicit                           
      187 -role, an explicit confirmation and values inside fixed limits.
          - \textbf{R5}~(honesty): simulated or                          
      188 -unavailable data must never look like a live measurement. \tex
          -tbf{R6}~(accessibility): the full                             
      189 -experiment must be rehearsable on a single student laptop with
          -out virtual machines.                                         
      235  
      191 -\subsection{Laboratory Topology}                              
      192 -Fig.~\ref{fig:topology} shows the topology. The untrusted WAN 
          -segment (10.10.10.0/24) contains the                          
      193 -traffic source, and the protected LAN segment (192.168.10.0/24
          -) contains a web server (the                                  
      194 -``victim''), a DNS server, a workstation and the monitoring ho
          -st that runs the console. Both                                
      195 -segments use private RFC~1918 address space \cite{rfc1918}, wh
          -ich cannot be routed on the public                            
      196 -Internet. pfSense is the only path between the segments, so a 
          -single WAN rule is enough to                                  
      197 -protect a LAN host.                                           
      236 +Six requirements shaped the design. \textbf{R1}~(isolation): a
          +ll                                                            
      237 +generated traffic must stay inside the authorized network.    
      238 +\textbf{R2}~(measurability): the effect of one rule must be   
      239 +expressible as before/after numbers. \textbf{R3}~(explainabili
          +ty):                                                          
      240 +a student must be able to trace exactly why a packet was allow
          +ed                                                            
      241 +or blocked. \textbf{R4}~(safe by default): dangerous actions r
          +equire                                                        
      242 +an explicit administrator role, explicit confirmation and valu
          +es                                                            
      243 +inside hard limits. \textbf{R5}~(honesty): simulated or unavai
          +lable                                                         
      244 +data must never be presented as a live measurement.           
      245 +\textbf{R6}~(accessibility): the full experiment must be rehea
          +rsable                                                        
      246 +on a single laptop without virtual machines.

      229 -\subsection{Software Architecture}                            
      230 -The console is split into four parts. A \emph{backend} exposes
          - the laboratory model (hosts, rules,                          
      231 -tests, results, events, alerts and the audit log) over a REST 
          -API and streams telemetry, events                             
      232 -and test status over an authenticated WebSocket once per secon
          -d. A \emph{provider} interface                                
      233 -hides where telemetry comes from. The Demo provider produces s
          -imulated values. The Lab provider                             
      234 -defines the integration point for reading pfSense status throu
          -gh SNMP \cite{rfc3411} and filter                             
      235 -logs through syslog \cite{rfc5424}; in the current version it 
          -is a stub that reports every value                            
      236 -as unavailable until such a source is connected, rather than i
          -nventing numbers. A \emph{test controller} enforces that only 
          -one test runs at a time and                                   
      237 -ends every test with two independent timers: one for the reque
          -sted duration and a hard backstop                             
      238 -at the configured maximum. Finally, a separate \emph{traffic w
          -orker} is intended to run on the                              
      239 -Kali host in Lab Mode. The backend itself never executes a pac
          -ket tool.

          -' phase. Because evaluation is                                
      244 -first-match, the rule has to sit \emph{above} the allow rules 
          -\#100 and \#101; placed below                                 
      245 -them it would never match web or ICMP traffic to the victim.

      271 -Algorithm~\ref{alg:eval} shows the evaluator used by the conso
          -le's \emph{policy tester}. For each                           
      272 -packet it walks the rules in ascending rule number, skips disa
          -bled rules and rules bound to a                               
      273 -different interface, and compares protocol, source, destinatio
          -n and port. Addresses may be                                  
      274 -\texttt{any}, a single IPv4 address, a CIDR block or a comma-s
          -eparated list; ports may be a                                 
      275 -single port, a list or a range. The function returns the verdi
          -ct, the deciding rule and a trace                             
      276 -entry for every rule it examined, including the reason a rule 
          -did not match. Its cost is linear in                          
      277 -the number of rules.

      279 -\begin{algorithm}[t]                                          
      280 -\caption{First-match policy evaluation with trace}            
      281 -\label{alg:eval}                                              
      282 -\small                                                        
      283 -\begin{algorithmic}[1]                                        
      284 -\Require rules $R$, packet $p = (\mathit{if}, \mathit{proto}, 
          -\mathit{src}, \mathit{dst}, \mathit{port})$                   
      285 -\State $T \gets [\,]$                                         
      286 -\For{$r \in R$ sorted by rule number}                         
      287 -  \If{$r$ is disabled} \State append $(r, \text{skipped-disabl
          -ed})$ to $T$; \textbf{continue} \EndIf                        
      288 -  \If{$r.\mathit{if} \neq p.\mathit{if}$} \State append $(r, \
          -text{skipped-interface})$ to $T$; \textbf{continue} \EndIf    
      289 -  \State $M \gets$ list of fields of $p$ that $r$ does not mat
          -ch                                                            
      290 -  \If{$M = \emptyset$}                                        
      291 -    \State append $(r, \text{match})$ to $T$                  
      292 -    \State \Return $(r.\mathit{action}, r, T)$                
      293 -  \EndIf                                                      
      294 -  \State append $(r, \text{no-match}, M)$ to $T$              
      295 -\EndFor                                                       
      296 -\State \Return $(\text{BLOCK}, \bot, T)$ \Comment{implicit def
          -ault deny}                                                    
      297 -\end{algorithmic}                                             
      298 -\end{algorithm}

      300 -\subsection{Threshold-Based Detection}                        
      301 -Every second the monitor reads the latest telemetry sample $k$
          - and derives two rates: the traffic                           
      302 -that passed the firewall, $r_k = o_k - b_k$, where $o_k$ is th
          -e observed and $b_k$ the blocked                              
      303 -packet rate, and the blocked rate $b_k$ itself. Given a sustai
          -n window of $w$ seconds and                                   
      304 -thresholds $\theta_r$ and $\theta_b$, a \emph{high-traffic-rat
          -e} alert is raised when

      460 +                                                              
      461 +\subsection{Threshold Detection}                              
      462 +                                                              
      463 +Every second the monitor reads the current telemetry sample $k
          +$ and                                                         
      464 +derives two rates: traffic passed to the LAN ($r_k = o_k - b_k
          +$,                                                            
      465 +where $o_k$ is observed and $b_k$ blocked), and the blocked ra
          +te                                                            
      466 +$b_k$ itself. A \emph{high-traffic-rate} alert fires when     
      467  \begin{equation}
      306 -  \min_{j \in \{k-w+1,\dots,k\}} r_j > \theta_r ,             
      307 -  \label{eq:detect}                                           
      468 +  \min_{j \in \{k-w+1,\ldots,k\}} r_j > \theta_r ,            
      469  \end{equation}
      309 -and a \emph{firewall-drop-spike} alert is raised under the sam
          -e condition with $b_j$ and                                    
      310 -$\theta_b$. The severity is escalated from MEDIUM to HIGH when
          - $r_k > 2\theta_r$. While an alert                            
      311 -of a given type is still open, further findings of that type a
          -re suppressed; each new alert is                              
      312 -also written to the event log as a ``Threshold exceeded'' even
          -t. The defaults are                                           
      313 -$\theta_r = 1200$\,pps, $\theta_b = 500$\,pps and $w = 3$\,s. 
          -Administrators may change them within                         
      314 -fixed bounds, and every change is audited together with its pr
          -evious value. Requiring the                                   
      315 -condition to hold for all $w$ samples filters out one-second s
          -pikes, at the price of a detection                            
      316 -delay of at least $w$ seconds.                                
      470 +and a \emph{firewall-drop-spike} alert fires under the same co
          +ndition                                                       
      471 +with $b_j$ and threshold $\theta_b$. Severity escalates from M
          +EDIUM                                                         
      472 +to HIGH when $r_k > 2\theta_r$. While an alert of a given type
          + is                                                           
      473 +still open, further findings are suppressed. Defaults: $\theta
          +_r =                                                          
      474 +1200$\,pps, $\theta_b = 500$\,pps, $w = 3$\,s.                
      475  
      476  \subsection{Safety Controls}
      319 -Safety is enforced on the server, because the browser cannot b
          -e trusted. A test request passes                              
      320 -through a fixed sequence of checks: schema validation, explici
          -t operator confirmation, target                               
      321 -validation, protocol allowlist, duration cap and rate cap. The
          - target must be a syntactically                               
      322 -valid IPv4 address inside the configured laboratory allowlist 
          -(192.168.10.0/24 by default), and it                          
      323 -must not be a public, loopback or local address of the server 
          -itself. Only the ADMIN role may                               
      324 -start or stop tests or change rules; ANALYST users may acknowl
          -edge alerts and read audit logs, and                          
      325 -VIEWER users have read-only access. Login and test-control end
          -points are rate limited. In Lab                               
      326 -Mode the traffic worker re-validates every request against its
          - \emph{own} allowlist, calls tools                            
      327 -without a shell so that a target value cannot inject a command
          -, and only dry-runs until it is                               
      328 -explicitly armed. Every login, test, rule change, threshold ch
          -ange and alert action is recorded                             
      329 -in an audit log.                                              
      477  
      331 -\subsection{Provenance of Data}                               
      332 -Each telemetry value carries one of three provenance labels: \
          -emph{live}, \emph{simulated} or                               
      333 -\emph{unavailable}. The console shows them as small tags next 
          -to every number, and when a value is                          
      334 -unavailable it shows ``N/A'' instead of a placeholder figure. 
          -This addresses R5 and, in our view,                           
      335 -is the most important design rule of the whole system: a teach
          -ing tool that silently mixes                                  
      336 -simulated and real numbers would teach the wrong lesson.      
      478 +Safety is enforced on the server---the browser is never truste
          +d. A                                                          
      479 +test request must pass: schema validation, explicit operator  
      480 +confirmation, target validation (the target must be IPv4 insid
          +e the                                                         
      481 +lab allowlist, not public, loopback or the server itself), a p
          +rotocol                                                       
      482 +allowlist, a duration cap and a rate cap. Only the ADMIN role 
          +may                                                           
      483 +start or stop tests or change rules. Test-control endpoints ar
          +e                                                             
      484 +rate-limited. In Lab Mode the Kali worker re-validates against
          + its                                                          
      485 +own independent allowlist, invokes tools without a shell to pr
          +event                                                         
      486 +command injection, and runs in dry-run mode until explicitly a
          +rmed.

      339 -\section{Implementation}\label{sec:impl}

      490 +\subsection{Provenance}                                       
      491  
      342 -The system is a TypeScript monorepo of roughly 8\,900 lines: a
          - Node.js/Express backend, a                                   
      343 -React/Vite frontend and the Lab-Mode traffic worker. Persisten
          -ce uses the SQLite engine that is                             
      344 -built into Node.js (\texttt{node:sqlite}) with versioned migra
          -tions, each applied inside a single                           
      345 -transaction, and parameterised queries throughout. Choosing th
          -e built-in engine removed every                               
      346 -native build step, which mattered on student laptops. Sessions
          - use JSON Web Tokens                                          
      347 -\cite{rfc7519} and passwords are hashed with scrypt \cite{rfc7
          -914}.                                                         
      492 +Each telemetry value carries one of three tags: \emph{live},  
      493 +\emph{simulated} or \emph{unavailable}. When a value is unavai
          +lable,                                                        
      494 +the console shows ``N/A'' rather than inventing a number. This
          + is the                                                       
      495 +most important design decision in the whole system. A teaching
          + tool                                                         
      496 +that silently mixes modelled and measured values would teach t
          +he wrong                                                      
      497 +lesson about what evidence is.                                
      498  
      349 -The console has twelve pages arranged as the chapters of a lab
          - report: \emph{Observe} (overview,                            
      350 -topology, traffic), \emph{Defend} (firewall and policy tester,
          - controlled tests),                                           
      351 -\emph{Analyse} (events, alerts, reports), \emph{Learn} (a lab 
          -manual with a short self-check quiz)                          
      352 -and \emph{Administer} (audit log, system status, settings). Re
          -ports are laid out as an academic                             
      353 -document with an aim, the experimental setup, a results table 
          -and chart, and a conclusion, and                              
      354 -can be exported as CSV, JSON or a printable page.

      356 -To give every page realistic content on first start, a seeding

          -ved), an audit trail and 24\,h of                             
      359 -per-minute traffic rollups. A seeded pseudo-random generator m
          -akes the dataset reproducible, which                          
      360 -the test suite relies on.                                     
      504 +The system is roughly 8,900 lines of TypeScript across a Node.
          +js/Express                                                    
      505 +backend, a React/Vite frontend and the Lab-Mode Kali worker.  
      506 +Persistence uses the SQLite engine built into Node.js         
      507 +(\texttt{node:sqlite}), with versioned migrations applied insi

          +n engine

          +t

      366 -management, the policy tester, event search and export, alert

      542  \subsection{Environment}
      374 -All measurements were taken on a laptop with an AMD Ryzen 9 59
          -00HX processor (8 cores, 16                                   
      375 -threads) and 16\,GB of RAM, running Windows 11 Pro and Node.js
          - 26.4.0. The console ran in Demo                              
      376 -Mode.

          +ine

      548  \subsection{Measured versus Modelled Quantities}
      379 -Because the evaluation uses Demo Mode, it is important to stat
          -e precisely what each number                                  
      380 -represents. The packet counts and the victim's service metrics
          - (availability, packet loss, latency                          
      381 -and CPU load) are produced by the \emph{simulation engine}. Th
          -ey show that the platform behaves as                          
      382 -designed, but they are not measurements of pfSense or of a rea
          -l server. In particular, the                                  
      383 -victim's metrics in the ``before'' phase are a deterministic f
          -unction of the configured rate and                            
      384 -intensity, which is why that column in Table~\ref{tab:results}
          - has zero variance. By contrast, the                          
      385 -detection delay, the false-alarm count, the evaluator verdicts
          -, the evaluator performance and the                           
      386 -test results are properties of the implemented software, and w
          -e report them as measurements.                                
      549  
      388 -\subsection{Experimental Protocol}                            
      389 -One repetition of the experiment has three phases against the 
          -victim (192.168.10.10):                                       
      550 +This distinction matters and must be stated clearly. The victi
          +m's                                                           
      551 +availability, packet loss, latency and CPU figures come from t
          +he                                                            
      552 +Demo-Mode simulation engine, not from pfSense or a real server
          +. They                                                        
      553 +confirm the platform works as designed. The detection delays, 
          +policy                                                        
      554 +evaluator verdicts, benchmark numbers and test pass/fail resul
          +ts are                                                        
      555 +measurements of the implemented software. Rows in the results 
          +tables                                                        
      556 +below are labelled accordingly.                               
      557 +                                                              
      558 +\subsection{Protocol}                                         
      559 +                                                              
      560 +One repetition covers three phases against the victim         
      561 +(192.168.10.10):                                              
      562  \begin{itemize}
      391 -  \item \textbf{P1 (baseline):} ICMP, 100\,pps cap, 10\,s, low
          - intensity;                                                   
      392 -  \item \textbf{P2 (load, no rule):} TCP, 3000\,pps cap, 15\,s
          -, high intensity, rule \#50 disabled;                         
      393 -  \item \textbf{P3 (load, rule applied):} the same parameters 
          -as P2 with rule \#50 enabled.                                 
      563 +\item \textbf{P1 (baseline)}: ICMP, 100\,pps cap, 10\,s, low  
      564 +      intensity;                                              
      565 +\item \textbf{P2 (load, no rule)}: TCP, 3000\,pps cap, 15\,s, 
          +high                                                          
      566 +      intensity, rule\,\#50 disabled;                         
      567 +\item \textbf{P3 (load, rule applied)}: identical parameters w
          +ith                                                           
      568 +      rule\,\#50 enabled.                                     
      569  \end{itemize}
      395 -Phases were separated by 4\,s of idle time. A script drove the
          - public REST API exactly as the                               
      396 -console does and repeated the experiment five times. Before ea
          -ch repetition it acknowledged all                             
      397 -open alerts, so that alert deduplication could not hide the al
          -erts of the new repetition. The                               
      398 -detection delay is the difference between the alert timestamp 
          -and the start time of the phase.                              
      399 -The detector used its default thresholds.                     
      570 +Phases are separated by 4\,s of idle time. A script drives the
      571 +public REST API exactly as the console does, and repeats the f
          +ull                                                           
      572 +three-phase sequence five times. Before each repetition all op
          +en                                                            
      573 +alerts are acknowledged, so deduplication cannot suppress aler
          +ts                                                            
      574 +belonging to that run.                                        
      575  
      401 -For the evaluator benchmark we called the evaluation function 
          -in-process, after 2\,000 warm-up                              
      402 -calls, in five batches of 50\,000 calls on the seeded policy, 
          -and took the median batch. To study                           
      403 -scaling we built worst-case policies of $n = 10, 100$ and $100
          -0$ rules that never match the test                            
      404 -packet, followed by a default deny, so that every rule had to 
          -be examined.                                                  
      576 +For the evaluator benchmark, the evaluation function is called
      577 +in-process after 2,000 warm-up calls, in five batches of 50,00
          +0                                                             
      578 +calls on the seeded nine-rule policy, taking the median batch.
          + To                                                           
      579 +probe scaling, three worst-case policies are constructed with 
          +$n =                                                          
      580 +10$, 100 and 1000 rules that never match the probe packet, so 
          +every                                                         
      581 +rule must be examined before the default deny fires.          
      582

       766  \subsection{Detection}
       536 -The high-traffic-rate alert was raised in all five P2 phases,
           - on average $3.38 \pm 0.22$\,s after the                     
       537 -phase started (range 3.07--3.64\,s). This matches the design:
           - the 3\,s sustain window of                                  
       538 -(\ref{eq:detect}) plus the short ramp-up of the load and the 
           -alignment with the 1\,Hz sampling                            
       539 -clock. In every P3 phase the firewall-drop-spike alert follow
           -ed after $2.87 \pm 0.31$\,s, while the                       
       540 -high-traffic-rate condition was not met because the traffic p
           -assing the firewall stayed far below                         
       541 -$\theta_r$. No high-traffic-rate alert occurred during any of
           - the five baseline phases. Five                              
       542 -repetitions are too few for general claims about false-alarm 
           -rates, but the outcome is the                                
       543 -expected one: baseline traffic of about 60\,pps is twenty tim
           -es smaller than the default                                  
       544 -threshold.                                                   
       767  
       546 -\subsection{Policy Evaluation}                               
       547 -Table~\ref{tab:probes} lists the verdicts of the evaluator fo
           -r nine representative packets, with                          
       548 -rule \#50 disabled and enabled. All verdicts agree with a man
           -ual reading of                                               
       549 -Table~\ref{tab:rules}. The table also exposes a shadowing eff
           -ect that is easy to miss: once \#50 is                       
       550 -enabled it matches every WAN packet from 10.10.10.0/24 to the
           - victim, so it shadows \#100, \#101                          
       551 -and, for that host, \#120. A connection to port~445, which ha
           -d been \emph{rejected} by \#120, is now                      
       552 -silently \emph{blocked} by \#50. Traffic to the DNS server (1
           -92.168.10.30) is unaffected because                          
       553 -\#50 is scoped to the victim's address.                      
       768 +The high-traffic-rate alert fired in all five P2 phases, on a
           +verage                                                       
       769 +$3.38 \pm 0.22$\,s after phase start (range 3.07--3.64\,s). T
           +his                                                          
       770 +matches expectations: the 3-second sustain window plus the ra
           +mp-up                                                        
       771 +time of the simulated load plus discretisation at the 1\,Hz s
           +ampling                                                      
       772 +clock. In P3 the firewall-drop-spike alert followed at $2.87 
           +\pm                                                          
       773 +0.31$\,s, while the high-traffic-rate condition was not trigg
           +ered                                                         
       774 +(the load passing the firewall stayed well below $\theta_r$).
           + No                                                          
       775 +alert of any type appeared during any of the five baseline ph
           +ases.                                                        
       776 +Five repetitions cannot establish a general false-alarm rate,
           + but                                                         
       777 +the outcome is the expected one: 60\,pps baseline traffic is 
       778 +twenty times smaller than the default threshold.

       579 -On the seeded policy one evaluation took a median of 4.68\,$\
           -mu$s; for the web packet in

           -all. Parsing each rule once in

           -e rule-set sizes used in teaching,

           -econd refresh interval of the

       588 -\subsection{Functional Testing}                              
       589 -All 81 automated tests passed. The backend suite ran in about
           - 2\,s and the frontend suite in under                        
       590 -1\,s. The suite includes negative tests for every guardrail, 
           -for example a public target                                  
       591 -(rejected with HTTP 422), an unconfirmed request (400), a tes
           -t started by a non-administrator                             
       592 -(403), a duplicate rule number (409) and an attempt to delete
           - a built-in rule (403). Seventeen                            
       593 -manual test cases cover the remaining interactive behaviour.

       852 +dashed line is a linear fit ($1.7\,\mu$s / rule). The seeded 
           +nine-rule                                                    
       853 +policy at $4.7\,\mu$s is well left of the chart minimum.}    
       854 +\label{fig:scaling}                                          
       855 +\end{figure}

       890 +%%  VII. DISCUSSION AND VALIDITY THREATS

       892 +\section{Discussion and Validity Threats}\label{sec:discussio
           +n}                                                           
       893 +                                                             
       894  \subsection{What the Results Do and Do Not Show}
       600 -The results show that the platform reproduces the expected \e
           -mph{shape} of the experiment: the                            
       601 -same offered load either reaches the protected host or is abs
           -orbed at the edge, depending on one                          
       602 -rule, and the console makes this visible within seconds. They
           - do not show how much a real pfSense                         
       603 -installation protects a real server. Those numbers depend on 
           -hardware, on the services being                              
       604 -protected and on the traffic mix, and can only come from Lab 
           -Mode. We have kept the two kinds of                          
       605 -numbers apart throughout this paper, and the console does the
           - same through its provenance tags.                           
       895  
       607 -\subsection{A Rule-Order Error Found in Our Own Design}      
       608 -In the first version of the laboratory the mitigation rule wa
           -s seeded as rule \#900, below the                            
       609 -allow rules. The simulation did not evaluate the rule set, so
           - the experiment still appeared to                            
       610 -work. When the first-match evaluator was added, the policy te
           -ster showed at once that web traffic                         
       611 -to the victim was decided by \#101 and that \#900 could never
           - match it. On a real pfSense                                 
       612 -installation the ``after'' phase would therefore have failed.
           - We moved the rule to \#50 and added                         
       613 -a database migration for existing installations. This is prec
           -isely the kind of error reported by                          
       614 -Wool \cite{wool2004quantitative}, and finding it in our own w
           -ork was a useful reminder of why rule                        
       615 -order deserves an explicit place in teaching material.       
       896 +The results confirm the platform's behaviour is correct and  
       897 +consistent across five runs. What they do not show is how muc
           +h a                                                          
       898 +physical pfSense installation protects a real server under re
           +al                                                           
       899 +load. Those numbers depend on hardware, the services being   
       900 +protected and the traffic mix. We have kept the two categorie
           +s                                                            
       901 +completely separate---the console enforces this with its prov
           +enance                                                       
       902 +tags, and this paper does the same.                          
       903  
       617 -\subsection{Threats to Validity}                             
       618 -The victim metrics are modelled, and the model is simple: it 
           -is linear in the offered rate and                            
       619 -deterministic in the ``before'' phase. All measurements were 
           -taken on one machine and one                                 
       620 -operating system, with five repetitions. Detection delays are
           - quantised by the 1\,Hz sampling                             
       621 -interval. We also observed that timings taken at the HTTP lev
           -el on this Windows machine clustered                         
       622 -at about 15.6\,ms, which matches the operating system's defau
           -lt timer resolution rather than the                          
       623 -cost of the work being done, so we did not report them. Final
           -ly, we have not run a classroom                              
       624 -study, so we make no claim about learning gains.             
       904 +\subsection{The Rule-Order Bug We Found in Our Own Work}     
       905  
       626 -% ===========================================================
           -================                                             
       627 -\section{Ethical Considerations}\label{sec:ethics}           
       628 -% ===========================================================
           -================                                             
       906 +In the first version of the laboratory, the mitigation rule w
           +as                                                           
       907 +seeded as rule\,\#900, below the allow rules. The simulation 
           +engine                                                       
       908 +at that point did not evaluate the rule set---it just toggled
           + a                                                           
       909 +flag---so the experiment appeared to work correctly. When we 
           +added                                                        
       910 +the first-match evaluator and ran it against the seeded polic
           +y, it                                                        
       911 +showed immediately that web traffic to the victim was decided
           + by                                                          
       912 +rule\,\#101 and that rule\,\#900 could never match it. On a r
           +eal                                                          
       913 +pfSense installation the ``after'' phase would therefore have
           + shown                                                       
       914 +no improvement at all. The fix was to move the rule to positi
           +on                                                           
       915 +\#50 and add a database migration for existing installations.
       916  
       630 -High-rate traffic generation is dual-use, so the laboratory i
           -s designed to make misuse difficult.                         
       631 -Demo Mode sends no packets at all. In Lab Mode traffic may on
           -ly be directed at addresses inside a                         
       632 -private allowlist, rates and durations are capped, only admin
           -istrators may start a test, every                            
       633 -request must be explicitly confirmed, and every action is log
           -ged. The laboratory must be operated                         
       634 -only on isolated networks owned by the institution and with t
           -he authorisation recommended by                              
       635 -NIST SP~800-115 \cite{nist800115}. The scope is intentionally
           - limited to a single-source,                                 
       636 -rate-capped flow against a designated host, which is enough t
           -o teach the defensive principle                              
       637 -without providing a general-purpose attack tool.             
       917 +This is exactly the type of error Wool                       
       918 +documented~\cite{wool2004quantitative}. Finding it in our own
           + work                                                        
       919 +was useful confirmation that the evaluator and its trace serv
           +e                                                            
       920 +their intended purpose.                                      
       921  
       639 -% ===========================================================
           -================                                             
       640 -\section{Conclusion and Future Work}\label{sec:conclusion}   
       641 -% ===========================================================
           -================                                             
       922 +\subsection{ATT\&CK Technique Mapping}                       
       923  
       643 -We presented a teaching laboratory that lets students observe
           -, in numbers, what one pfSense rule                          
       644 -does for a protected host under load, while keeping generated
           - traffic inside an isolated network                          
       645 -through layered, server-side controls. An explainable first-m
           -atch evaluator makes rule order and                          
       646 -shadowing visible, a transparent threshold detector raises al
           -erts with a predictable delay, and                           
       647 -provenance tags keep simulated and live data apart. In five r
           -epetitions in Demo Mode, enabling the                        
       648 -mitigation rule removed 93.4\% of the offered load at the edg
           -e, alerts arrived after about 3.4\,s                         
       649 -with no false alarms in the baseline phases, and the evaluato
           -r remained in the microsecond range                          
       650 -for realistic policies.                                      
       924 +Our rule\,\#50 targets a single attacker address and blocks a
           +ll                                                           
       925 +protocols. This maps to MITRE T1498 (Network Denial of Servic
           +e)                                                           
       926 +at the defence side. A production rule would likely be more  
       927 +surgical---rate-based with SYN-proxy for TCP or ICMP rate lim
           +its.                                                         
       928 +We chose the bluntest effective rule so the before/after cont
           +rast                                                         
       929 +is as clear as possible for teaching.                        
       930  
       652 -Four directions remain open. First, the Lab provider has to b
           -e completed (SNMP polling of                                 
       653 -interface counters and parsing of the pfSense filter log) and
           - a measurement campaign run on                               
       654 -virtualised and physical pfSense installations, to replace th
           -e modelled victim metrics. Second, a classroom study should t
           -est whether the laboratory improves students'                
       655 -understanding of rule order. Third, the policy tester could w
           -arn about shadowed and redundant                             
       656 -rules automatically, following the anomaly classes of \cite{a
           -lshaer2004discovery}. Fourth,                                
       657 -compiling rules once in advance would make the evaluator fast
           - enough for much larger policies.
